\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})=\sum_{i,j,k,l,m=1}^{s}b_{i}a_{ij}a_{ik}a_{kl}a_{km}=\sum_{i,k=1}^{s}b_{i}c_{i}a_{ik}c_{k}^{2}.

The algebraic reformulation of Section
3
allows us to take over the classical order theory of Runge-Kutta methods for ODEs
[
36
]
and to adapt it in the context of approximations of formal solutions to Hamilton-Jacobi equation. The following theorem provides new Hamiltonian Poisson integrators.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let us consider a RKHP method (
5.3
) with coefficient map
a\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
and set
e\in\mathcal{T}^{*}
as in the equation (
3.6
). Then, if
a(\tau)=e(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{N}
, the method has order
N
for solving (
2.1
).
Moreover, under the degeneracy condition (
3.7
), if
a(\tau)=e(\tau)
for all
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{0}^{N}
, the method has order
N
for solving (
2.1
).

Theorem
5.3
allows us to recover the standard order conditions of Runge-Kutta methods
[
36
, Chap. III]
in the general case. Moreover, we provide in Table
1
the order conditions in the degenerate case (
3.7
). These tree degeneracies are new to the best of our knowledge.

Table 1:
Order conditions of RKHP integrators under the degeneracy condition (
3.7
).

Order

\text{Butcher tree }\tau

\text{Order condition }a(\tau)=e(\tau)

1

\sum_{i=1}^{s}b_{i}=1

3

\sum_{i=1}^{s}b_{i}c_{i}^{2}=\frac{1}{3}

4

\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}b_{i}a_{ij}c_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{12}

\sum_{i=1}^{s}b_{i}c_{i}^{3}=\frac{1}{4}

5

\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{s}b_{i}a_{ij}a_{jk}c_{k}^{2}=\frac{1}{60}

\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}b_{i}a_{ij}c_{j}^{3}=\frac{1}{20}

\sum_{i,j=1}^{s}b_{i}c_{i}a_{ij}c_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{15}

\sum_{i=1}^{s}b_{i}c_{i}^{4}=\frac{1}{5}

Remark 5.4
.

The idea of approximating bi-realisations at high order has been explored by
[
11
]
, where a procedure to asymptotically compute the source map
\alpha\colon\mathcal{G}\to M
is investigated. This relies on a long history research of deformation theory in order to construct the local symplectic groupoid of any Poisson manifold
[
16
,
10
]
. We expect a relation between the order of the approximation of the bi-realisation and the one of the dynamics to provide robust numerical methods.

In the case of a general bi-realisation, a collection of explicit RKHP integrators can be derived straightforwardly from the standard Runge-Kutta integrators from
[
36
]
.
In the case of a bi-realisation satisfying (
3.7
), we propose a collection of new methods with minimal number of stages for fixed order. The superconvergence property of Theorem
3.13
allows us to reach high order
p
with less than
p
evaluations.
We emphasize that there is no need to consider discretisations associated to symplectic methods (like the midpoint method) as the geometry has been taken care of by the bi-realisations.

Euler method:

The Euler method is a first order RKHP method (resp. second order under (
3.7
)).

\begin{array}[]{rl}y_{n}&=\alpha(\Delta t\cdot d_{x_{n}}H)\\
y_{n+1}&=\beta(\Delta t\cdot d_{x_{n}}H)\end{array}\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{c|c}0&0\\
\hline\cr&1\end{array}

Third order method:

Under (
3.7
), third order can be achieved with two stages.

\begin{array}[]{rl}Z&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\Delta t\cdot dH\\
y_{n}&=\alpha(\Delta t\cdot d_{x_{n}}Z^{*}\alpha^{*}H)\\
y_{n+1}&=\beta(\Delta t\cdot d_{x_{n}}Z^{*}\alpha^{*}H)\end{array}\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{c|cc}0&0&0\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
\hline\cr&0&1\end{array}

Fourth order method:

Under (
3.7
), fourth order can be achieved with three stages, that is one stage fewer than for the popular RK4 discretisation.

\begin{array}[]{rl}Z_{1}&=-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\Delta t\cdot dH\\
Z_{2}&=\frac{3}{4}\Delta t\cdot dZ_{1}^{*}\alpha^{*}H\\
y_{n}&=\alpha\left(\Delta t(\frac{11}{27}d_{x_{n}}H+\frac{16}{27}d_{x_{n}}Z_{2}^{*}\alpha^{*}H)\right)\\
y_{n+1}&=\beta\left(\Delta t(\frac{11}{27}d_{x_{n}}H+\frac{16}{27}d_{x_{n}}Z_{2}^{*}\alpha^{*}H)\right)\end{array}\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}0&0&0&0\\
-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}&-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}&0&0\\
\frac{3}{4}&0&\frac{3}{4}&0\\
\hline\cr&\frac{11}{27}&0&\frac{16}{27}\end{array}

Remark 5.5
.

If implicit implementations are computationally feasible and (
3.7
) is satisfied, a one-stage third order implicit method is

\begin{array}[]{rl}Z&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\Delta t\cdot dZ^{*}\alpha^{*}H\\
y_{n}&=\alpha(\Delta t\cdot d_{x_{n}}Z^{*}\alpha^{*}H)\\
y_{n+1}&=\beta(\Delta t\cdot d_{x_{n}}Z^{*}\alpha^{*}H)\end{array}\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{c|c}\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\\
\hline\cr&1\end{array}

Remark 5.6
.

The Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra used in Section
4
is also relevant to approximations of bisections in
\mathcal{G}
. Since they admit a group law, a computational consequence is the existence of composition methods.
For instance, let
\xi\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G}),

\zeta_{0}\in\Omega^{1,\mathcal{G}}(M)
and let the first order approximation of the Farmer series, analogous to the Euler method, be given by

\psi^{t\xi}(\zeta_{0}):=\zeta_{0}+t\zeta_{0}^{*}\xi=\zeta_{0}+B^{\xi}\bigl(\delta_{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to2.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.22394pt\lower-1.22394pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.28 0 C 1.28 0.71 0.71 1.28 0 1.28 C -0.71 1.28 -1.28 0.71 -1.28 0 C -1.28 -0.71 -0.71 -1.28 0 -1.28 C 0.71 -1.28 1.28 -0.71 1.28 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.28}{0.0}{0.0}{0.28}{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.28 0.0 0.0 0.28 0 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\bigr)(\zeta_{0}).

Then the explicit midpoint method is the composition

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}^{t\xi}(\zeta_{0})

\displaystyle:=(\psi^{t\xi}\circ\psi^{t\xi/2})(\zeta_{0})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{0}+t\xi\Bigl(\zeta_{0}+\frac{t}{2}\cdot\zeta_{0}^{*}\xi\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{0}+B^{\xi}\Bigl(\delta_{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to2.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.22394pt\lower-1.22394pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.28 0 C 1.28 0.71 0.71 1.28 0 1.28 C -0.71 1.28 -1.28 0.71 -1.28 0 C -1.28 -0.71 -0.71 -1.28 0 -1.28 C 0.71 -1.28 1.28 -0.71 1.28 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.28}{0.0}{0.0}{0.28}{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.28 0.0 0.0 0.28 0 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}*(\delta_{\hbox to2.45pt{\vbox to2.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.22394pt\lower-1.22394pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.6pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.28 0 C 1.28 0.71 0.71 1.28 0 1.28 C -0.71 1.28 -1.28 0.71 -1.28 0 C -1.28 -0.71 -0.71 -1.28 0 -1.28 C 0.71 -1.28 1.28 -0.71 1.28 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.28}{0.0}{0.0}{0.28}{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.28 0.0 0.0 0.28 0 0)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}/2)\Bigr)(\zeta_{0})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{0}+t\zeta_{0}^{*}\xi+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\zeta_{0}^{*}[\xi,\pi^{*}\zeta_{0}^{*}\xi]+\frac{t^{3}}{8}\zeta_{0}^{*}[[\xi,\pi^{*}\zeta_{0}^{*}\xi],\pi^{*}\zeta_{0}^{*}\xi]+\dots

We observe in particular that
\hat{\psi}
provides a second order approximation for a general
\xi\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G})
, similarly to the context of ODEs.

6
Conclusion

The algebraic tools of geometric integration extend for the study of Poisson geometry. In the framework of symplectic groupoids, they bring new insights, from both geometric and computational viewpoints.

Some stability properties of low order Hamiltonian Poisson integrators have been studied in
[
22
]
. The present work allows one to implement high order Hamiltonian Poisson integrators as well. We also plan to implement implicit methods and benchmark their orders in order to provide numerical illustrations of our algebraic results. Steep dynamical systems of conservative mechanics may benefit from the formalism we introduced; those ideas will deserve a more mechanics oriented article.

On the algebraic side, we expect this work to open many perspectives. A natural extension could adapt the Hopf algebra of substitution for the backward error analysis in this context and to pave the way towards the combinatorial foundations of Hamiltonian Poisson integrators. At last, a more general geometric context could yield post-Lie algebras.
This is matter for future work.
